When I hear that word, I usually picture someone who is, you know, constantly worrying about the future, or fretting over whether they left the stove on, or stressing about paying the electric bill on time. My cousin, for instance, she has terrible anxiety, and she’s always wringing her hands and talking about how she’s sure something bad is going to happen to her kids at school, or she can't sleep because she's overthinking a conversation she had three days ago. \n\nBut I don't really have that. I'm not sitting around worrying about things like that. My mind isn't spinning with all these \"what ifs,\" you know? It's not really nervousness. It's more just... like I was saying before with the stockroom thing, it's just this intense, heavy pressure. Like a rubber band that's been stretched as far as it can possibly go and is just waiting to snap. So I wouldn't say I feel anxious, exactly. I don't have that nervous energy where I'm scared of everyday things. It's just this weird, horrible tension all the time, and I don't know why it's there, but it doesn't feel like worry. Does that make sense? Because people always throw that word around, \"oh I'm so anxious,\" but I really don't think that's what this is. I just feel wound up.\n\nDoctor: Would you describe yourself as a perfectionistic person?\n\nPatient: Perfectionistic? Um... no, I really wouldn't say that. I mean, I like things done right, obviously, especially at work. Like, if you're putting out a display for the new spring line, you want the colors to coordinate and the tags to be tucked away so it looks nice for the customers. That's just doing your job well, you know? But I'm not one of those people who obsesses over every little detail being absolutely flawless. \n\nMy sister-in-law, now she is a perfectionist. When she hosted Thanksgiving last year, she literally ironed the cloth napkins. Ironed them! And she had a complete meltdown because the gravy boat didn't exactly match the trim on the dinner plates. It was exhausting just watching her. I'm definitely not like that. If something is good enough, it's good enough for me. My apartment is clean, but there's probably some mail piled up on the counter right now, and the bed isn't made like a hotel room, and that doesn't bother me at all. I don't need everything to be perfect. So, no, I wouldn't use that word for myself. I just try to be practical and get things done, but I don't lose sleep over things not being picture-perfect.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any recurring thoughts or mental images that keep coming back to you, even when you try to ignore them?\n\nPatient: Well, actually... yes. Yes, I do, and it's honestly driving me crazy. It's the fire. I mean, I haven't mentioned it yet, but there was a fire at my apartment building about a month ago. It started in the unit right below mine, in their kitchen, and it spread so fast. I was home, and... anyway, that's what keeps coming back. It just jumps into my head completely out of nowhere. \n\nIt's not like I'm sitting there dwelling on it or trying to remember it. It's the exact opposite. I'll be doing something totally normal, completely unrelated. Like yesterday morning, I was just making breakfast. I went to pick up a frying pan to make some eggs, just a normal Tuesday morning, and the second my hand touched the handle, it was like I wasn't in my kitchen anymore. I was right back in the middle of it. I could literally smell the smoke—that awful, thick, acrid smell that just coats the back of your throat—and I could see the smoke just billowing up through my floorboards and filling up the room, getting thicker and thicker. \n\nAnd it happens at work, too! I'll be sitting in the back office, pulling up an inventory spreadsheet on the computer, just staring at the numbers, and suddenly, boom, the screen is gone and I'm seeing the flames shooting up the side of the building outside my window. It's so vivid, it's like a movie playing in my head that I can't turn off. I try to shake it away, I'll literally blink my eyes hard and shake my head to try and clear it, but it happens before I can even try to stop it. It just hijacks my brain. And then my heart starts racing and I get that horrible tight feeling in my chest, and it takes me ten minutes just to realize I'm safe in the store and not back in that burning building. It's just so exhausting, having it pop up like that when I'm just trying to go about my day.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding places or activities that remind you of the fire?\n\nPatient: Avoidance? Oh, yeah. Definitely. I mean, it's gotten to the point where I'm practically rearranging my entire life just so I don't have to deal with anything that even remotely makes me think about that day. \n\nLike... the house, obviously. I haven't been back. I can't. Even just the thought of driving down that street, seeing the boarded-up windows or the scorch marks on the brick—I just can't do it. My sister had to go grab the few things that weren't ruined because every time I even thought about going over there, my chest would get so tight I felt like I couldn't breathe. \n\nBut it's not just the building itself. It's... it's the weirdest little things. Like, my mortgage folder. I keep all my important documents in this blue accordion folder, right? And I know I need to look at it, I need to call the bank, I need to deal with the insurance stuff, but every time I even look at that blue folder sitting on my sister's dining table, my stomach just drops. I can't even touch it. It's like it's radioactive or something. Just knowing that it's tied to the house, to the fire, it makes me feel sick. I just walk right past it and pretend it's not there.\n\nAnd... this is going to sound so stupid, but even the herbs on my patio. Well, not *my* patio anymore, but I had this little setup with basil and rosemary, and I loved it. I would go out there every morning. And the other day, I was at the grocery store, just in the produce section, and I walked past the fresh herbs. The smell of the rosemary hit me, and instantly, I was right back there, remembering how I was watering them the morning of the fire, right before everything went wrong. I literally dropped my basket and had to leave the store. I couldn't even be in the same aisle. \n\nIt's just exhausting. I feel like I'm constantly on guard, trying to figure out what's going to trigger that horrible tight feeling in my chest next. So, yeah, if I can avoid it, I do. I just can't make myself go near anything that brings it all back. I just can't.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself being overly alert or on the lookout for potential dangers, even when you're in a safe environment?\n\nPatient: Overly alert? Um... no, I wouldn't really say that. I mean, I'm definitely stressed out, obviously, and my mind is going a mile a minute with all the things I'm trying not to think about, but I don't feel like I'm constantly scanning the room for danger or anything like that. \n\nLike, when I'm at my sister's house, I know I'm safe. I'm not sitting there checking the smoke detectors or watching the stove like a hawk or jumping at every little noise. I'm just sitting on her couch, trying to watch TV and ignore that blue folder on the table. And at work, I'm just trying to get through my shift. I'm not looking around the store wondering if something bad is going to happen. I mean, the store is perfectly safe. It's a brand new building, they just updated all the fire suppression systems last year, actually, because corporate made a big push for safety compliance across all the regional branches. The regional manager, Dave, he actually came down and did this whole walk-through with the fire marshal, and they checked every single sprinkler head and fire extinguisher. It took like three hours, and we had to keep pausing the floor reset we were doing in the men's department. \n\nSo, no, I know I'm perfectly safe when I'm there. I'm not on the lookout for anything. It's just... it's the memories that are the problem, not what's actually happening around me right now. I don't feel like I'm in danger in the present moment, if that makes sense. I just can't escape what already happened. But as far as being hyper-vigilant or whatever you want to call it, looking out for new dangers? No, I haven't really noticed myself doing that. I'm just exhausted from dealing with the stuff that's already in my head.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're outside of your body, watching yourself, or like things aren't real?\n\nPatient: Yes, actually. It's so strange that you ask that, because I was just trying to explain this to my sister the other day and I couldn't even find the right words for it. I thought I was completely losing my mind. \n\nIt happens a lot when I'm at work. I'll just be walking through the store—like, the other day it was a Thursday, right around two o'clock, which is usually our mid-afternoon rush when the local high school gets out and all the older folks are finishing up their errands. I was walking out from the back stockroom carrying this huge stack of denim, because we just got the new fall line in and we had to set up this whole new display table over by the fitting rooms. Anyway, I'm walking through the main aisle, and suddenly... it's like I'm not even in my own body anymore. \n\nIt literally feels like I'm watching myself from a few feet away. Like I'm hovering just behind my own right shoulder, just observing this woman who looks exactly like me carrying a stack of jeans. And everything around me—the aisles, the bright fluorescent lights, the customers picking through the clearance racks—they're all there, I know they're physically there, but they don't seem quite real. It's like they're flat, almost like I'm walking through a movie set or a photograph, and there's this thick pane of glass between me and the rest of the world. \n\nI'm just moving through them on complete automatic pilot. My legs are walking, I'm dodging around this lady with a massive stroller in the activewear section, I'm even smiling and nodding at people as I pass by, but I'm not actually *doing* it. My body is just doing it on its own while I float a few feet away watching it happen. I remember finally getting to the display table and setting the jeans down, and I just stood there staring at my own hands, trying to figure out if they actually belonged to me. It's just the most bizarre, unsettling feeling in the world, feeling so completely disconnected from yourself and your surroundings like that. It took me a good twenty minutes of just standing by the registers before things started feeling solid and real again.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these episodes of intense anger and flashbacks to the fire?\n\nPatient: Two weeks. It's been exactly... well, about two weeks now. Ever since the kitchen fire. \n\nIt's actually kind of a cruel joke, honestly, because it happened during our very first weekend working on the house. We had just gotten the keys that Thursday, and I had already taken Friday off work so we could get a head start. I had this whole schedule planned out, you know? We were going to start with the kitchen because it had this awful, peeling wallpaper from like, the 1970s, and this really sticky linoleum floor that drove me crazy just looking at it. We spent the whole Saturday just scraping and scrubbing, and we were so exhausted but so happy. We were ordering takeout and sitting on folding chairs, and it felt like this amazing fresh start. \n\nAnd then... the fire happened. \n\nSo, yeah, for the last two weeks, ever since that weekend, it's just been... relentless. The flashbacks just pull me right back into that kitchen, right back into the smoke and the panic, like I'm standing there all over again. And the anger... God, the anger is just exhausting. I've never been an angry person, but lately, I'll just be sitting there and suddenly I'm so mad I'm physically shaking. I'm just furious that our first weekend in our new home got destroyed like that, that everything we were working for just went up in smoke before we even really got to start. It's so unfair. So it's been two weeks of this. Two straight weeks of feeling like I'm completely losing my grip on everything, and I just can't seem to make it stop.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty or responsible in any way for the fire that occurred at your apartment building?\n\nPatient: Guilty? No. No, not at all. And actually, just to clarify, it was a house we had just bought, not an apartment building. But no, I don't feel responsible in the slightest, because there was literally nothing I could have done to prevent it. It wasn't like I left a stove burner on or knocked over a candle or did something careless. I'm actually incredibly meticulous about safety things like that. Like at work, I'm always the one who makes sure the space heaters in the breakroom are fully unplugged and the cords are wrapped up at the end of my shift. \n\nWhat actually happened—and we know this for a fact because the fire investigator, this really thorough guy from the county, came out a few days later and dug through all the debris—was an electrical fault hidden completely inside the wall. He said the previous owners must have done some unpermitted DIY wiring years ago. They had spliced these wires together with just cheap electrical tape behind the drywall, right near the baseboards where the old refrigerator used to sit. So when we were cleaning up all that scraped wallpaper I mentioned earlier, we plugged our heavy-duty shop vac into that outlet, and it just overloaded that faulty junction. \n\nWe didn't know! How could we have known? We paid four hundred and fifty dollars for a premium home inspection before we even closed on the house, and the inspector completely missed it because it was sealed up behind the wall. So if anyone should feel guilty, it's the people who did the shoddy wiring in the first place, or maybe the inspector who gave us the all-clear and took our money. But me? No. I was just standing there with a putty knife in my hand when the smoke started pouring out of the outlet. I didn't do anything wrong. I'm just the one who has to pay the price for it now, dealing with all this anger and the memories and the kitchen being totally gutted. But I definitely don't feel like it was my fault.\n\nDoctor: Do you avoid talking or thinking about your feelings related to the fire?\n\nPatient: Well... yes and no. I mean, obviously, I'm talking to you about it right now, but... in my day-to-day life? I just can't. I completely shut it down. \n\nIt's not just talking about it, either. It's... it's everything. Like, I’ve been completely avoiding the house. We're staying at my sister's place right now, sleeping on this horribly uncomfortable pull-out couch in her basement, and my husband keeps saying we need to go back to the house to meet with the insurance adjuster and the contractors, but I just... I can't. I literally can't make myself drive over there. The thought of pulling into that driveway and seeing the boarded-up window where the kitchen used to be... my chest just seizes up. It gets so tight I feel like I can't breathe, and my heart starts pounding so hard I can hear it in my ears. \n\nAnd it's not just the house itself. It's anything even remotely connected to it. I have this thick manila folder—the mortgage folder, with all our closing documents and the deed and the home insurance policy—and it's sitting on the coffee table at my sister's house, and I won't even touch it. My husband asked me to find the insurance policy number the other day, and I just looked at the folder and started shaking. I had to walk out of the room. I can't even look at the little potted herbs I brought over from our patio. I had this beautiful little basil plant and some rosemary that I was so excited to put in the new kitchen window, and now... if I even smell them, if I even catch a whiff of that rosemary, I'm right back in that smoke. \n\nSo I just avoid it all. I don't look at the folder, I don't go near the plants, I definitely don't go near the house, and if my husband tries to bring up the repairs or how I'm feeling about it, I just change the subject or find an excuse to leave the room. I know it's probably driving him crazy, and I know we have to deal with it eventually, but right now... anything tied to that day, anything at all, just makes my chest tighten up so badly that I just can’t make myself go near it. I just want to pretend it never happened, even though I know that's impossible.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like you're in a daze or disconnected from the world around you?\n\nPatient: Yes. Actually, yes, that is exactly how it feels. I went back to work last week—I work retail, at this big department store over at the mall, usually in the home goods section, which is honestly a little hard right now with everything going on with our house, but I couldn't afford to take any more time off. But anyway, when I'm there... it's so strange. Sometimes when I’m walking through the store, it literally feels like I’m watching myself from a few feet away. \n\nLike, I can see my own hands folding the towels or fixing a display of throw pillows, but I'm not really, I don't know, *inside* my body doing it. And the whole environment around me... the aisles, the bright fluorescent lights, the customers pushing their carts and asking me where the clearance rack is... they're there, I know they're physically there, but they just don't feel quite real. It's like I'm walking through a movie set, or like there's this thick layer of glass between me and everything else. \n\nI'm just moving through them on automatic. I'll be at the register ringing someone up, and I'll hear my own voice saying, \"Did you find everything okay today?\" and \"Your total is forty-two fifty,\" but it doesn't even feel like it's *me* talking. It's just my mouth moving while the real me is floating somewhere up near the ceiling. My manager even asked me on Tuesday if I was coming down with a cold, because I guess I was just standing there staring blankly at an endcap of coffee makers for God knows how long. I didn't even realize I was doing it. I just... I get so completely detached from everything. It’s exhausting, honestly, feeling like a ghost in your own life.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like you're living in a dream or that things around you aren't real?\n\nPatient: Yes, exactly! That is exactly what I was just trying to explain about being at work. It really is like a dream, but... but a really dull, exhausting one that you can't wake up from. \n\nLike I was just saying a second ago, when I'm walking through the store, it literally feels like I'm watching myself from a few feet away. I'm there, but I'm not really *there*. The aisles, the displays, the customers asking me questions—they are all around me, but they just aren't quite real. It's like they're made of cardboard, or like I'm watching them on a television screen with the volume turned down low. And I'm just moving through them completely on automatic. \n\nYou know how when you drive somewhere you've been a million times, and you get to your destination and suddenly realize you don't actually remember the drive? It's exactly like that, but for my entire shift. I'll just be floating there, watching my own hands scan items or fold a stack of bath towels, and feeling like absolutely none of it is actually happening. It's the most bizarre, unsettling feeling in the world, because I know I'm awake, I know I had my morning coffee, I know I physically clocked in... but my brain just won't accept that any of the world around me is actually real right now. It's like I'm just a robot going through the motions while the real me is trapped somewhere else.\n\nDoctor: Do you try to avoid thinking about the fire or your feelings related to it?\n\nPatient: I know I kind of poured all this out a few minutes ago, but yes, absolutely. I try my hardest to just completely block it all out. Because the second I let myself actually think about the fire, or how terrified I am, it's like my body just completely betrays me. \n\nThat's exactly why I’ve been avoiding the house so much, like I mentioned earlier. It’s not just that I don't want to see the damage—though obviously I don't—it's that going there forces me to actually process what happened, and I just can't do it. And it extends to literally anything that might trigger a thought about it. That mortgage folder I was telling you about, the one sitting on my sister's coffee table? I will literally walk all the way around the outside edge of the living room just so I don't have to look at it, because looking at it means thinking about the insurance, which means thinking about the fire. \n\nAnd even those little herbs from my patio... the basil and the rosemary. I thought I was doing a good thing bringing them, you know? Saving something from the mess. But now, if I even catch a whiff of them, my brain goes right back to that day, and my chest just tightens up so horribly. It gets so tight, like there's a heavy concrete block sitting right on my ribs, and I just can’t make myself go near any of it. Not the house, not the paperwork, not the plants. I just push the thoughts as far down as I possibly can, because if I don't avoid them, I feel like I won't be able to breathe.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I know we just talked about this, and I feel like I'm repeating myself, but yes, it's pretty much constant. It's exactly what I was trying to explain about being at work. It's like... it's like I'm stuck in this weird, foggy bubble all the time now. 

I'll be walking through the home goods section, right? And I'll be looking at the rows of blenders and the stacks of placemats, and it literally feels like I'm hovering a few feet outside of my own body, just watching myself walk down the aisle. Like I'm a character in a video game and someone else is holding the controller. The customers will be right there in front of me, asking me if we have a specific brand of sheets in stock, and I can hear them, I can see them, but they just don't feel... real. They feel like props. The whole store feels like a prop. 

And I'm just moving through it all on complete automatic pilot. I'll be smiling and pointing toward aisle four, but inside, I'm just completely detached from the whole interaction. It's not just at work, either, it's everywhere, but work is where I notice it the most because I'm supposed to be so 'on' and present for the customers. But I'm just... not. I'm just watching myself go through the motions from somewhere else. It's so exhausting, feeling like you're not even really inside your own life anymore.

It's the most bizarre, unsettling feeling in the world, because I know I'm awake, I know I had my morning coffee, I know I physically clocked in... but my brain just won't accept that any of the world around me is actually real right now. It's like I'm just a robot going through the motions while the real me is trapped somewhere else.\n\nDoctor: Do you try to avoid thinking about the fire or your feelings related to it?\n\nPatient: I know I kind of poured all this out a few minutes ago, but yes, absolutely. It gets so tight, like there's a heavy concrete block sitting right on my ribs, and I just can’t make myself go near any of it. Not the house, not the paperwork, not the plants. I just push the thoughts as far down as I possibly can, because if I don't avoid them, I feel like I won't be able to breathe.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're living in a dream or detached from reality most of the time?\n\nPatient: I know we just talked about this, and I feel like I'm repeating myself, but yes, it's pretty much constant. Oh, it's... it's affecting absolutely everything. I mean, my whole life is just completely falling apart right now. I’ve always been the responsible one, you know? And then on top of work being a total disaster, there's everything with the house. Like I was saying before, I just absolutely cannot face going back to the damaged house. I know there are things I'm supposed to be doing—meeting with the adjusters, sorting through whatever is left in the rubble, filling out all those terrible forms—but I physically cannot make myself drive over there. 

So because I'm avoiding the house, and I'm barely functioning at the store, all of my work and home responsibilities are just piling up faster than I can handle. It’s like this massive, suffocating mountain of tasks and phone calls and decisions, and I'm just paralyzed. My manager is leaving me voicemails, the insurance company is emailing me, the mail is stacking up on my sister's kitchen counter, and I just... I can't do any of it. I'm just watching it all pile up and I have no idea how I'm ever going to dig myself out.

It gets so tight I feel like I can't breathe, and my heart starts pounding so hard I can hear it in my ears. \n\nAnd it's not just the house itself. It's anything even remotely connected to it. I have this thick manila folder—the mortgage folder, with all our closing documents and the deed and the home insurance policy—and it's sitting on the coffee table at my sister's house, and I won't even touch it. My husband asked me to find the insurance policy number the other day, and I just looked at the folder and started shaking. I had to walk out of the room. I can't even look at the little potted herbs I brought over from our patio. I had this beautiful little basil plant and some rosemary that I was so excited to put in the new kitchen window, and now... if I even smell them, if I even catch a whiff of that rosemary, I'm right back in that smoke. \n\nSo I just avoid it all. I don't look at the folder, I don't go near the plants, I definitely don't go near the house, and if my husband tries to bring up the repairs or how I'm feeling about it, I just change the subject or find an excuse to leave the room. I know it's probably driving him crazy, and I know we have to deal with it eventually, but right now... anything tied to that day, anything at all, just makes my chest tighten up so badly that I just can’t make myself go near it. I just want to pretend it never happened, even though I know that's impossible.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like you're in a daze or disconnected from the world around you?\n\nPatient: Yes. Actually, yes, that is exactly how it feels. I went back to work last week—I work retail, at this big department store over at the mall, usually in the home goods section, which is honestly a little hard right now with everything going on with our house, but I couldn't afford to take any more time off. But anyway, when I'm there... it's so strange. Sometimes when I’m walking through the store, it literally feels like I’m watching myself from a few feet away. \n\nLike, I can see my own hands folding the towels or fixing a display of throw pillows, but I'm not really, I don't know, *inside* my body doing it. I didn't even realize I was doing it. I just... I get so completely detached from everything. I'm there, but I'm not really *there*. The aisles, the displays, the customers asking me questions—they are all around me, but they just aren't quite real. It's like they're made of cardboard, or like I'm watching them on a television screen with the volume turned down low. And I'm just moving through them completely on automatic. \n\nYou know how when you drive somewhere you've been a million times, and you get to your destination and suddenly realize you don't actually remember the drive? I'm just watching myself go through the motions from somewhere else. My manager is leaving me voicemails, the insurance company is emailing me, the mail is stacking up on my sister's kitchen counter, and I just... I can't do any of it. It's like I'm just a robot going through the motions while the real me is trapped somewhere else.\n\nDoctor: Do you try to avoid thinking about the fire or your feelings related to it?\n\nPatient: I know I kind of poured all this out a few minutes ago, but yes, absolutely. I try my hardest to just completely block it all out. Because the second I let myself actually think about the fire, or how terrified I am, it's like my body just completely betrays me. \n\nThat's exactly why I’ve been avoiding the house so much, like I mentioned earlier. It’s not just that I don't want to see the damage—though obviously I don't—it's that going there forces me to actually process what happened, and I just can't do it. And it extends to literally anything that might trigger a thought about it. That mortgage folder I was telling you about, the one sitting on my sister's coffee table? I will literally walk all the way around the outside edge of the living room just so I don't have to look at it, because looking at it means thinking about the insurance, which means thinking about the fire. \n\nAnd even those little herbs from my patio... the basil and the rosemary. I thought I was doing a good thing bringing them, you know? Saving something from the mess. But now, if I even catch a whiff of them, my brain goes right back to that day, and my chest just tightens up so horribly. It gets so tight, like there's a heavy concrete block sitting right on my ribs, and I just can’t make myself go near any of it. Not the house, not the paperwork, not the plants. I just push the thoughts as far down as I possibly can, because if I don't avoid them, I feel like I won't be able to breathe.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're living in a dream or detached from reality most of the time?\n\nPatient: I know we just talked about this, and I feel like I'm repeating myself, but yes, it's pretty much constant. It's exactly what I was trying to explain about being at work. It's like... it's like I'm stuck in this weird, foggy bubble all the time now. \n\nI'll be walking through the home goods section, right? And I'll be looking at the rows of blenders and the stacks of placemats, and it literally feels like I'm hovering a few feet outside of my own body, just watching myself walk down the aisle. Like I'm a character in a video game and someone else is holding the controller. The customers will be right there in front of me, asking me if we have a specific brand of sheets in stock, and I can hear them, I can see them, but they just don't feel... real. They feel like props. The whole store feels like a prop. \n\nAnd I'm just moving through it all on complete automatic pilot. I'll be smiling and pointing toward aisle four, but inside, I'm just completely detached from the whole interaction. It's not just at work, either, it's everywhere, but work is where I notice it the most because I'm supposed to be so 'on' and present for the customers. But I'm just... not. I'm just watching myself go through the motions from somewhere else. It's so exhausting, feeling like you're not even really inside your own life anymore.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any problems with your relationships or daily activities since the fire happened?\n\nPatient: Oh, it's... it's affecting absolutely everything. I mean, my whole life is just completely falling apart right now. I’ve always been the responsible one, you know? I show up on time, I handle my business, but lately... well, just the other day at the store, I completely missed a vendor meeting. It was this big meeting with the reps for a new cookware line we're supposed to be featuring for the holidays, and I knew about it, it was on my schedule, but I just... I couldn't make myself go. I was standing by the stockroom doors and I just couldn't do it. And then, I think it was Tuesday, it got so bad that I actually just walked out before finishing my shift. I didn't even tell my manager or ask someone to cover my section, I just grabbed my purse out of the breakroom locker and walked out the front doors because I couldn't take another second of pretending everything was normal. \n\nAnd then on top of work being a total disaster, there's everything with the house. My manager is leaving me voicemails, the insurance company is emailing me, the mail is stacking up on my sister's kitchen counter, and I just... I can't do any of it. I know I keep saying this, and I feel like a broken record, but yes. It’s exactly like I was saying before about being at the store. It’s this constant, heavy fog that I just can’t shake. 

Like, I’ll be walking down the main aisle, right past the seasonal displays—right now it’s all the autumn wreaths and the pumpkin spice candles that smell so strong they usually give me a headache—and I’ll just stop. And it feels... it literally feels like I’m floating just a few feet behind myself. Like I’m watching this woman in a blue polo shirt and a nametag walk past the candles, and I know it’s me, but it doesn’t *feel* like me. 

And the customers... they’ll come up to me, holding a throw pillow or asking where the bathroom is, and they look like people, they sound like people, but my brain is just telling me they’re not real. They’re like... like cardboard cutouts or something. The whole store feels like a movie set that I accidentally wandered onto. I’ll answer their questions, I’ll point them toward the restrooms, but I’m completely on autopilot. I’m just going through the motions, totally disconnected from my own mouth moving. It’s so bizarre and it’s honestly terrifying because I can’t snap myself out of it, no matter how hard I try to just focus on the placemats or the blenders. I’m just... not really there.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n16\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF43.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, honestly, I... I guess I’m here because I just keep blowing up at people over absolutely nothing, and it’s really starting to scare me. I work in retail, I’ve been in sales for years, and you know, you have to be patient in that line of work. You deal with all kinds of personalities. I've always prided myself on being the calm one, the one who can de-escalate things. But lately, it’s like my fuse is just completely gone. \n\nLike, just the other day—Tuesday, I think it was—we got a big shipment in, and one of the newer employees, she put some of the new stock on the wrong shelf in the backroom. It was a simple mistake, really, just a mix-up with the SKUs, but I just... I completely lost it. I yelled at her. I mean, I really screamed at her right there in the stockroom. And the terrible thing is, right afterward, I had to go hide in the employee restroom because I was shaking. I was literally trembling all over, just leaning against the sink looking at my hands shaking, because that is just not how I usually treat people. I am never, ever like that. I'm not a yeller. But it's like I have this intense anger just bubbling right under the surface all the time now, and I can't control it, and I feel so awful about how I'm acting but I don't know how to make it stop.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling anxious a lot lately?\n\nPatient: Anxious? Um... I don't really think so? I mean, I guess it depends on what you mean by anxious. When I hear that word, I usually picture someone who is, you know, constantly worrying about the future, or fretting over whether they left the stove on, or stressing about paying the electric bill on time. My cousin, for instance, she has terrible anxiety, and she’s always wringing her hands and talking about how she’s sure something bad is going to happen to her kids at school, or she can't sleep because she's overthinking a conversation she had three days ago. \n\nBut I don't really have that. I'm not sitting around worrying about things like that. My mind isn't spinning with all these \"what ifs,\" you know? It's not really nervousness. It's more just... like I was saying before with the stockroom thing, it's just this intense, heavy pressure. Like a rubber band that's been stretched as far as it can possibly go and is just waiting to snap. So I wouldn't say I feel anxious, exactly. I don't have that nervous energy where I'm scared of everyday things. It's just this weird, horrible tension all the time, and I don't know why it's there, but it doesn't feel like worry. Does that make sense? Because people always throw that word around, \"oh I'm so anxious,\" but I really don't think that's what this is. I just feel wound up.\n\nDoctor: Would you describe yourself as a perfectionistic person?\n\nPatient: Perfectionistic? Um... no, I really wouldn't say that. I mean, I like things done right, obviously, especially at work. Like, if you're putting out a display for the new spring line, you want the colors to coordinate and the tags to be tucked away so it looks nice for the customers. That's just doing your job well, you know? But I'm not one of those people who obsesses over every little detail being absolutely flawless. \n\nMy sister-in-law, now she is a perfectionist. When she hosted Thanksgiving last year, she literally ironed the cloth napkins. Ironed them! And she had a complete meltdown because the gravy boat didn't exactly match the trim on the dinner plates. It was exhausting just watching her. I'm definitely not like that. If something is good enough, it's good enough for me. My apartment is clean, but there's probably some mail piled up on the counter right now, and the bed isn't made like a hotel room, and that doesn't bother me at all. I don't need everything to be perfect. So, no, I wouldn't use that word for myself. I just try to be practical and get things done, but I don't lose sleep over things not being picture-perfect.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any recurring thoughts or mental images that keep coming back to you, even when you try to ignore them?\n\nPatient: Well, actually... yes. Yes, I do, and it's honestly driving me crazy. It's the fire. I mean, I haven't mentioned it yet, but there was a fire at my apartment building about a month ago. It started in the unit right below mine, in their kitchen, and it spread so fast. I was home, and... anyway, that's what keeps coming back. It just jumps into my head completely out of nowhere. \n\nIt's not like I'm sitting there dwelling on it or trying to remember it. It's the exact opposite. I'll be doing something totally normal, completely unrelated. Like yesterday morning, I was just making breakfast. I went to pick up a frying pan to make some eggs, just a normal Tuesday morning, and the second my hand touched the handle, it was like I wasn't in my kitchen anymore. I was right back in the middle of it. I could literally smell the smoke—that awful, thick, acrid smell that just coats the back of your throat—and I could see the smoke just billowing up through my floorboards and filling up the room, getting thicker and thicker. \n\nAnd it happens at work, too! I'll be sitting in the back office, pulling up an inventory spreadsheet on the computer, just staring at the numbers, and suddenly, boom, the screen is gone and I'm seeing the flames shooting up the side of the building outside my window. It's so vivid, it's like a movie playing in my head that I can't turn off. I try to shake it away, I'll literally blink my eyes hard and shake my head to try and clear it, but it happens before I can even try to stop it. It just hijacks my brain. And then my heart starts racing and I get that horrible tight feeling in my chest, and it takes me ten minutes just to realize I'm safe in the store and not back in that burning building. It's just so exhausting, having it pop up like that when I'm just trying to go about my day.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding places or activities that remind you of the fire?\n\nPatient: Avoidance? Oh, yeah. Definitely. I mean, it's gotten to the point where I'm practically rearranging my entire life just so I don't have to deal with anything that even remotely makes me think about that day. \n\nLike... the house, obviously. I haven't been back. I can't. Even just the thought of driving down that street, seeing the boarded-up windows or the scorch marks on the brick—I just can't do it. My sister had to go grab the few things that weren't ruined because every time I even thought about going over there, my chest would get so tight I felt like I couldn't breathe. \n\nBut it's not just the building itself. It's... it's the weirdest little things. Like, my mortgage folder. I keep all my important documents in this blue accordion folder, right? And I know I need to look at it, I need to call the bank, I need to deal with the insurance stuff, but every time I even look at that blue folder sitting on my sister's dining table, my stomach just drops. I can't even touch it. It's like it's radioactive or something. Just knowing that it's tied to the house, to the fire, it makes me feel sick. I just walk right past it and pretend it's not there.\n\nAnd... this is going to sound so stupid, but even the herbs on my patio. Well, not *my* patio anymore, but I had this little setup with basil and rosemary, and I loved it. I would go out there every morning. And the other day, I was at the grocery store, just in the produce section, and I walked past the fresh herbs. The smell of the rosemary hit me, and instantly, I was right back there, remembering how I was watering them the morning of the fire, right before everything went wrong. I literally dropped my basket and had to leave the store. I couldn't even be in the same aisle. \n\nIt's just exhausting. I feel like I'm constantly on guard, trying to figure out what's going to trigger that horrible tight feeling in my chest next. So, yeah, if I can avoid it, I do. I just can't make myself go near anything that brings it all back. I just can't.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself being overly alert or on the lookout for potential dangers, even when you're in a safe environment?\n\nPatient: Overly alert? Um... no, I wouldn't really say that. I mean, I'm definitely stressed out, obviously, and my mind is going a mile a minute with all the things I'm trying not to think about, but I don't feel like I'm constantly scanning the room for danger or anything like that. \n\nLike, when I'm at my sister's house, I know I'm safe. I'm not sitting there checking the smoke detectors or watching the stove like a hawk or jumping at every little noise. I'm just sitting on her couch, trying to watch TV and ignore that blue folder on the table. And at work, I'm just trying to get through my shift. I'm not looking around the store wondering if something bad is going to happen. I mean, the store is perfectly safe. It's a brand new building, they just updated all the fire suppression systems last year, actually, because corporate made a big push for safety compliance across all the regional branches. The regional manager, Dave, he actually came down and did this whole walk-through with the fire marshal, and they checked every single sprinkler head and fire extinguisher. It took like three hours, and we had to keep pausing the floor reset we were doing in the men's department. \n\nSo, no, I know I'm perfectly safe when I'm there. I'm not on the lookout for anything. It's just... it's the memories that are the problem, not what's actually happening around me right now. I don't feel like I'm in danger in the present moment, if that makes sense. I just can't escape what already happened. But as far as being hyper-vigilant or whatever you want to call it, looking out for new dangers? No, I haven't really noticed myself doing that. I'm just exhausted from dealing with the stuff that's already in my head.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're outside of your body, watching yourself, or like things aren't real?\n\nPatient: Yes, actually. It's so strange that you ask that, because I was just trying to explain this to my sister the other day and I couldn't even find the right words for it. I thought I was completely losing my mind. \n\nIt happens a lot when I'm at work. I'll just be walking through the store—like, the other day it was a Thursday, right around two o'clock, which is usually our mid-afternoon rush when the local high school gets out and all the older folks are finishing up their errands. I was walking out from the back stockroom carrying this huge stack of denim, because we just got the new fall line in and we had to set up this whole new display table over by the fitting rooms. Like I'm hovering just behind my own right shoulder, just observing this woman who looks exactly like me carrying a stack of jeans. And everything around me—the aisles, the bright fluorescent lights, the customers picking through the clearance racks—they're all there, I know they're physically there, but they don't seem quite real. It's like they're flat, almost like I'm walking through a movie set or a photograph, and there's this thick pane of glass between me and the rest of the world. \n\nI'm just moving through them on complete automatic pilot. My legs are walking, I'm dodging around this lady with a massive stroller in the activewear section, I'm even smiling and nodding at people as I pass by, but I'm not actually *doing* it. My body is just doing it on its own while I float a few feet away watching it happen. I remember finally getting to the display table and setting the jeans down, and I just stood there staring at my own hands, trying to figure out if they actually belonged to me. It's just the most bizarre, unsettling feeling in the world, feeling so completely disconnected from yourself and your surroundings like that. We had just gotten the keys that Thursday, and I had already taken Friday off work so we could get a head start. I had this whole schedule planned out, you know? We were going to start with the kitchen because it had this awful, peeling wallpaper from like, the 1970s, and this really sticky linoleum floor that drove me crazy just looking at it. I've never been an angry person, but lately, I'll just be sitting there and suddenly I'm so mad I'm physically shaking. It wasn't like I left a stove burner on or knocked over a candle or did something careless. I'm actually incredibly meticulous about safety things like that. They had spliced these wires together with just cheap electrical tape behind the drywall, right near the baseboards where the old refrigerator used to sit. How could we have known? No. I was just standing there with a putty knife in my hand when the smoke started pouring out of the outlet. I didn't do anything wrong. I'm just the one who has to pay the price for it now, dealing with all this anger and the memories and the kitchen being totally gutted. I can't even touch it. It's like it's radioactive or something. Just knowing that it's tied to the house, to the fire, it makes me feel sick. I just walk right past it and pretend it's not there.\n\nAnd... this is going to sound so stupid, but even the herbs on my patio. Well, not *my* patio anymore, but I had this little setup with basil and rosemary, and I loved it. I would go out there every morning. And the other day, I was at the grocery store, just in the produce section, and I walked past the fresh herbs. The smell of the rosemary hit me, and instantly, I was right back there, remembering how I was watering them the morning of the fire, right before everything went wrong. I literally dropped my basket and had to leave the store. I couldn't even be in the same aisle. \n\nIt's just exhausting. I feel like I'm constantly on guard, trying to figure out what's going to trigger that horrible tight feeling in my chest next. So, yeah, if I can avoid it, I do. I just can't make myself go near anything that brings it all back. I just can't.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself being overly alert or on the lookout for potential dangers, even when you're in a safe environment?\n\nPatient: Overly alert? Um... no, I wouldn't really say that. I mean, I'm definitely stressed out, obviously, and my mind is going a mile a minute with all the things I'm trying not to think about, but I don't feel like I'm constantly scanning the room for danger or anything like that. \n\nLike, when I'm at my sister's house, I know I'm safe. I'm not sitting there checking the smoke detectors or watching the stove like a hawk or jumping at every little noise. I'm just sitting on her couch, trying to watch TV and ignore that blue folder on the table. And at work, I'm just trying to get through my shift. I'm not looking around the store wondering if something bad is going to happen. I mean, the store is perfectly safe. It's a brand new building, they just updated all the fire suppression systems last year, actually, because corporate made a big push for safety compliance across all the regional branches. The regional manager, Dave, he actually came down and did this whole walk-through with the fire marshal, and they checked every single sprinkler head and fire extinguisher. It took like three hours, and we had to keep pausing the floor reset we were doing in the men's department. \n\nSo, no, I know I'm perfectly safe when I'm there. I'm not on the lookout for anything. It's just... it's the memories that are the problem, not what's actually happening around me right now. I don't feel like I'm in danger in the present moment, if that makes sense. I just can't escape what already happened. But as far as being hyper-vigilant or whatever you want to call it, looking out for new dangers? No, I haven't really noticed myself doing that. I'm just exhausted from dealing with the stuff that's already in my head.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're outside of your body, watching yourself, or like things aren't real?\n\nPatient: Yes, actually. It's so strange that you ask that, because I was just trying to explain this to my sister the other day and I couldn't even find the right words for it. I thought I was completely losing my mind. \n\nIt happens a lot when I'm at work. I'll just be walking through the store—like, the other day it was a Thursday, right around two o'clock, which is usually our mid-afternoon rush when the local high school gets out and all the older folks are finishing up their errands. I was walking out from the back stockroom carrying this huge stack of denim, because we just got the new fall line in and we had to set up this whole new display table over by the fitting rooms. Anyway, I'm walking through the main aisle, and suddenly... it's like I'm not even in my own body anymore. \n\nIt literally feels like I'm watching myself from a few feet away. Like I'm hovering just behind my own right shoulder, just observing this woman who looks exactly like me carrying a stack of jeans. And everything around me—the aisles, the bright fluorescent lights, the customers picking through the clearance racks—they're all there, I know they're physically there, but they don't seem quite real. It's like they're flat, almost like I'm walking through a movie set or a photograph, and there's this thick pane of glass between me and the rest of the world. \n\nI'm just moving through them on complete automatic pilot. My legs are walking, I'm dodging around this lady with a massive stroller in the activewear section, I'm even smiling and nodding at people as I pass by, but I'm not actually *doing* it. My body is just doing it on its own while I float a few feet away watching it happen. I remember finally getting to the display table and setting the jeans down, and I just stood there staring at my own hands, trying to figure out if they actually belonged to me. It's just the most bizarre, unsettling feeling in the world, feeling so completely disconnected from yourself and your surroundings like that. It took me a good twenty minutes of just standing by the registers before things started feeling solid and real again.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these episodes of intense anger and flashbacks to the fire?\n\nPatient: Two weeks. It's been exactly... well, about two weeks now. Ever since the kitchen fire. \n\nIt's actually kind of a cruel joke, honestly, because it happened during our very first weekend working on the house. We had just gotten the keys that Thursday, and I had already taken Friday off work so we could get a head start. I had this whole schedule planned out, you know? We were going to start with the kitchen because it had this awful, peeling wallpaper from like, the 1970s, and this really sticky linoleum floor that drove me crazy just looking at it. We spent the whole Saturday just scraping and scrubbing, and we were so exhausted but so happy. We were ordering takeout and sitting on folding chairs, and it felt like this amazing fresh start. \n\nAnd then... the fire happened. \n\nSo, yeah, for the last two weeks, ever since that weekend, it's just been... relentless. The flashbacks just pull me right back into that kitchen, right back into the smoke and the panic, like I'm standing there all over again. And the anger... God, the anger is just exhausting. I've never been an angry person, but lately, I'll just be sitting there and suddenly I'm so mad I'm physically shaking. I'm just furious that our first weekend in our new home got destroyed like that, that everything we were working for just went up in smoke before we even really got to start. It's so unfair. So it's been two weeks of this. Two straight weeks of feeling like I'm completely losing my grip on everything, and I just can't seem to make it stop.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty or responsible in any way for the fire that occurred at your apartment building?\n\nPatient: Guilty? No. No, not at all. And actually, just to clarify, it was a house we had just bought, not an apartment building. But no, I don't feel responsible in the slightest, because there was literally nothing I could have done to prevent it. It wasn't like I left a stove burner on or knocked over a candle or did something careless. I'm actually incredibly meticulous about safety things like that. Like at work, I'm always the one who makes sure the space heaters in the breakroom are fully unplugged and the cords are wrapped up at the end of my shift. \n\nWhat actually happened—and we know this for a fact because the fire investigator, this really thorough guy from the county, came out a few days later and dug through all the debris—was an electrical fault hidden completely inside the wall. He said the previous owners must have done some unpermitted DIY wiring years ago. They had spliced these wires together with just cheap electrical tape behind the drywall, right near the baseboards where the old refrigerator used to sit. So when we were cleaning up all that scraped wallpaper I mentioned earlier, we plugged our heavy-duty shop vac into that outlet, and it just overloaded that faulty junction. \n\nWe didn't know! How could we have known? We paid four hundred and fifty dollars for a premium home inspection before we even closed on the house, and the inspector completely missed it because it was sealed up behind the wall. So if anyone should feel guilty, it's the people who did the shoddy wiring in the first place, or maybe the inspector who gave us the all-clear and took our money. But me? No. I was just standing there with a putty knife in my hand when the smoke started pouring out of the outlet. I didn't do anything wrong. I'm just the one who has to pay the price for it now, dealing with all this anger and the memories and the kitchen being totally gutted. But I definitely don't feel like it was my fault.\n\nDoctor: Do you avoid talking or thinking about your feelings related to the fire?\n\nPatient: Well... yes and no. I mean, obviously, I'm talking to you about it right now, but... in my day-to-day life? I just can't. I completely shut it down. \n\nIt's not just talking about it, either. It's... it's everything. Like, I’ve been completely avoiding the house. We're staying at my sister's place right now, sleeping on this horribly uncomfortable pull-out couch in her basement, and my husband keeps saying we need to go back to the house to meet with the insurance adjuster and the contractors, but I just... I can't. I literally can't make myself drive over there. The thought of pulling into that driveway and seeing the boarded-up window where the kitchen used to be... my chest just seizes up. It gets so tight I feel like I can't breathe, and my heart starts pounding so hard I can hear it in my ears. \n\nAnd it's not just the house itself. It's anything even remotely connected to it. I have this thick manila folder—the mortgage folder, with all our closing documents and the deed and the home insurance policy—and it's sitting on the coffee table at my sister's house, and I won't even touch it. My husband asked me to find the insurance policy number the other day, and I just looked at the folder and started shaking. I had to walk out of the room. I can't even look at the little potted herbs I brought over from our patio. I had this beautiful little basil plant and some rosemary that I was so excited to put in the new kitchen window, and now... if I even smell them, if I even catch a whiff of that rosemary, I'm right back in that smoke. \n\nSo I just avoid it all. I don't look at the folder, I don't go near the plants, I definitely don't go near the house, and if my husband tries to bring up the repairs or how I'm feeling about it, I just change the subject or find an excuse to leave the room. I know it's probably driving him crazy, and I know we have to deal with it eventually, but right now... anything tied to that day, anything at all, just makes my chest tighten up so badly that I just can’t make myself go near it. I just want to pretend it never happened, even though I know that's impossible.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like you're in a daze or disconnected from the world around you?\n\nPatient: Yes. Actually, yes, that is exactly how it feels. I went back to work last week—I work retail, at this big department store over at the mall, usually in the home goods section, which is honestly a little hard right now with everything going on with our house, but I couldn't afford to take any more time off. But anyway, when I'm there... it's so strange. Sometimes when I’m walking through the store, it literally feels like I’m watching myself from a few feet away. \n\nLike, I can see my own hands folding the towels or fixing a display of throw pillows, but I'm not really, I don't know, *inside* my body doing it. And the whole environment around me... the aisles, the bright fluorescent lights, the customers pushing their carts and asking me where the clearance rack is... they're there, I know they're physically there, but they just don't feel quite real. It's like I'm walking through a movie set, or like there's this thick layer of glass between me and everything else. \n\nI'm just moving through them on automatic. I'll be at the register ringing someone up, and I'll hear my own voice saying, \"Did you find everything okay today?\" and \"Your total is forty-two fifty,\" but it doesn't even feel like it's *me* talking. It's just my mouth moving while the real me is floating somewhere up near the ceiling. My manager even asked me on Tuesday if I was coming down with a cold, because I guess I was just standing there staring blankly at an endcap of coffee makers for God knows how long. I didn't even realize I was doing it. I just... I get so completely detached from everything. It’s exhausting, honestly, feeling like a ghost in your own life.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like you're living in a dream or that things around you aren't real?\n\nPatient: Yes, exactly! That is exactly what I was just trying to explain about being at work. It really is like a dream, but... but a really dull, exhausting one that you can't wake up from. \n\nLike I was just saying a second ago, when I'm walking through the store, it literally feels like I'm watching myself from a few feet away. I'm there, but I'm not really *there*. The aisles, the displays, the customers asking me questions—they are all around me, but they just aren't quite real. It's like they're made of cardboard, or like I'm watching them on a television screen with the volume turned down low. And I'm just moving through them completely on automatic. \n\nYou know how when you drive somewhere you've been a million times, and you get to your destination and suddenly realize you don't actually remember the drive? It's exactly like that, but for my entire shift. I'll just be floating there, watching my own hands scan items or fold a stack of bath towels, and feeling like absolutely none of it is actually happening. It's the most bizarre, unsettling feeling in the world, because I know I'm awake, I know I had my morning coffee, I know I physically clocked in... but my brain just won't accept that any of the world around me is actually real right now. It's like I'm just a robot going through the motions while the real me is trapped somewhere else.\n\nDoctor: Do you try to avoid thinking about the fire or your feelings related to it?\n\nPatient: I know I kind of poured all this out a few minutes ago, but yes, absolutely. I try my hardest to just completely block it all out. Because the second I let myself actually think about the fire, or how terrified I am, it's like my body just completely betrays me. \n\nThat's exactly why I’ve been avoiding the house so much, like I mentioned earlier. It’s not just that I don't want to see the damage—though obviously I don't—it's that going there forces me to actually process what happened, and I just can't do it. And it extends to literally anything that might trigger a thought about it. That mortgage folder I was telling you about, the one sitting on my sister's coffee table? I will literally walk all the way around the outside edge of the living room just so I don't have to look at it, because looking at it means thinking about the insurance, which means thinking about the fire. \n\nAnd even those little herbs from my patio... the basil and the rosemary. I thought I was doing a good thing bringing them, you know? Saving something from the mess. But now, if I even catch a whiff of them, my brain goes right back to that day, and my chest just tightens up so horribly. It gets so tight, like there's a heavy concrete block sitting right on my ribs, and I just can’t make myself go near any of it. Not the house, not the paperwork, not the plants. I just push the thoughts as far down as I possibly can, because if I don't avoid them, I feel like I won't be able to breathe.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're living in a dream or detached from reality most of the time?\n\nPatient: I know we just talked about this, and I feel like I'm repeating myself, but yes, it's pretty much constant. It's exactly what I was trying to explain about being at work. It's like... it's like I'm stuck in this weird, foggy bubble all the time now. \n\nI'll be walking through the home goods section, right? And I'll be looking at the rows of blenders and the stacks of placemats, and it literally feels like I'm hovering a few feet outside of my own body, just watching myself walk down the aisle. Like I'm a character in a video game and someone else is holding the controller. The customers will be right there in front of me, asking me if we have a specific brand of sheets in stock, and I can hear them, I can see them, but they just don't feel... real. They feel like props. The whole store feels like a prop. \n\nAnd I'm just moving through it all on complete automatic pilot. I'll be smiling and pointing toward aisle four, but inside, I'm just completely detached from the whole interaction. It's not just at work, either, it's everywhere, but work is where I notice it the most because I'm supposed to be so 'on' and present for the customers. But I'm just... not. I'm just watching myself go through the motions from somewhere else. It's so exhausting, feeling like you're not even really inside your own life anymore.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any problems with your relationships or daily activities since the fire happened?\n\nPatient: Oh, it's... it's affecting absolutely everything. I mean, my whole life is just completely falling apart right now. I’ve always been the responsible one, you know? I show up on time, I handle my business, but lately... well, just the other day at the store, I completely missed a vendor meeting. It was this big meeting with the reps for a new cookware line we're supposed to be featuring for the holidays, and I knew about it, it was on my schedule, but I just... I couldn't make myself go. I was standing by the stockroom doors and I just couldn't do it. And then, I think it was Tuesday, it got so bad that I actually just walked out before finishing my shift. I didn't even tell my manager or ask someone to cover my section, I just grabbed my purse out of the breakroom locker and walked out the front doors because I couldn't take another second of pretending everything was normal. \n\nAnd then on top of work being a total disaster, there's everything with the house. Like I was saying before, I just absolutely cannot face going back to the damaged house. I know there are things I'm supposed to be doing—meeting with the adjusters, sorting through whatever is left in the rubble, filling out all those terrible forms—but I physically cannot make myself drive over there. \n\nSo because I'm avoiding the house, and I'm barely functioning at the store, all of my work and home responsibilities are just piling up faster than I can handle. It’s like this massive, suffocating mountain of tasks and phone calls and decisions, and I'm just paralyzed. My manager is leaving me voicemails, the insurance company is emailing me, the mail is stacking up on my sister's kitchen counter, and I just... I can't do any of it. I'm just watching it all pile up and I have no idea how I'm ever going to dig myself out.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're living in a dream or detached from reality most of the time?\n\nPatient: I know I keep saying this, and I feel like a broken record, but yes. It’s exactly like I was saying before about being at the store. It’s this constant, heavy fog that I just can’t shake. \n\nLike, I’ll be walking down the main aisle, right past the seasonal displays—right now it’s all the autumn wreaths and the pumpkin spice candles that smell so strong they usually give me a headache—and I’ll just stop. And it feels... it literally feels like I’m floating just a few feet behind myself. Like I’m watching this woman in a blue polo shirt and a nametag walk past the candles, and I know it’s me, but it doesn’t *feel* like me. \n\nAnd the customers... they’ll come up to me, holding a throw pillow or asking where the bathroom is, and they look like people, they sound like people, but my brain is just telling me they’re not real. They’re like... like cardboard cutouts or something. The whole store feels like a movie set that I accidentally wandered onto. I’ll answer their questions, I’ll point them toward the restrooms, but I’m completely on autopilot. I’m just going through the motions, totally disconnected from my own mouth moving. It’s so bizarre and it’s honestly terrifying because I can’t snap myself out of it, no matter how hard I try to just focus on the placemats or the blenders. I’m just... not really there."
